--- Page 49 ---
IN THE SUPREME COURT OF INDIA
CRIMINAL APPELLATE JURISDICTION 
Criminal Appeal No(s). 1238/2015
STATE OF KARNATAKA                         Appellant(s)
VERSUS
A. SHANTHAKUMAR @ KUMAR                    Respondent(s)
O R D E R
1. Heard learned counsel for the parties.
2. This appeal impugns the judgment and
order of the High Court of Karnataka at
Bengaluru1 dated  29.01.2015  in  Criminal
Appeal No. 1245/2011 whereby the appeal of
the respondent against the order of the
trial court convicting and sentencing him
under Sections 498A and 302 of the Indian
Penal  Code,  1860  (for  short  ‘IPC’)  was
allowed in part, conviction under Sections
302 and 498-A of IPC was set aside but he
was  convicted  for  an  offence  punishable
under Section 306 IPC and sentenced to five
1 ‘High Court’
1

--- Page 50 ---
years of imprisonment.
3. Interestingly, the respondent has not
preferred an appeal whereas the State is in
appeal against the order of the High Court
to the extent it has acquitted the accused
of the charge of offences punishable under
Sections 302 and 498A IPC.
4. We have been informed by the learned
counsel  for  the  respondent  that  the
respondent has already served five years of
sentence awarded to him under Section 306
IPC.
5. The  prosecution  case,  inter  alia ,
rests on two dying declarations. The first
is recorded by the Investigating Officer
(for short ‘I.O.’) between 3:30pm and 4:00pm
on 10.06.2010 and the other is recorded half
an hour later by the Executive Magistrate at
about 4:30pm on the same day. Both dying
declarations indict the respondent of having
poured  kerosene  on  the  deceased  i.e.,
respondent’s wife and setting her on fire.
2

--- Page 51 ---
6. To substantiate the prosecution case
besides the witnesses who recorded the dying
declaration  and  carried  out  the
investigation, PW1, namely, the sister of
the  deceased,  who  had  accompanied  the
deceased to the hospital, was examined to
substantiate that there had been a demand of
dowry.
7. In her deposition, PW1 states that at
the  time  of  marriage  the  accused  was
provided Rs. 1 lac in cash and ornaments
worth  Rs.  2  lacs  were  provided  to  the
deceased. The accused and the deceased lived
without any issues and in harmony for four
months. Thereafter, the demand started and
the accused was pressing the deceased to
fetch more money from her father. Besides,
the accused had pledged ornaments of the
deceased. In her deposition, PW1 stated that
her daughter Savitha used to stay with the
deceased in the house of the accused and
that she came to know about the incident
through her. PW1 further states that when
3

--- Page 52 ---
she went to the house of the deceased/
accused she found the victim lying down and
upon inquiry from the victim she came to
know that the accused had poured kerosene on
her and set her on fire. PW1 states that she
had taken the deceased and admitted her to a
hospital at Dandupalya from where she was
shifted  to  another  hospital,  namely,
Victoria, where she ultimately succumbed to
the  injuries,  next  day.  During  cross-
examination, she admitted that her own house
is just a kilometer away from the house of
the deceased and that her daughter Savitha
most of the time used to stay with the
deceased  and  that  the  deceased  and  the
accused after marriage had lived cordially
for few months. During cross examination she
admitted that she had not witnessed her
father paying cash of Rs.1 lacs to the
accused. She, however, denied the suggestion
that her daughter Savitha had informed her
that deceased suffered burn injuries due to
bursting of gas stove while cooking food.
4

--- Page 53 ---
8. Prosecution also examined the mother
of the deceased, namely, PW2. She reiterated
what PW1 stated but interestingly neither
PW1 nor PW2 made any disclosure as to when
the accused made a demand and as to what was
the demand made by him. In fact, deposition
of PW1 and PW2 statements are identical in
respect  of  there  being  no  differences
between the accused and the deceased and
existence of cordial relations for a period
of 3-4 months. Though they allege that after
four months demand and harassment started,
they are silent as to what was the demand,
when it was raised and for what purpose.
9. The I.O., in his deposition, states
that  after  recording  of  the  dying
declaration of the deceased, he effected
arrest of the accused and had, thereafter,
taken the accused to the house where the
deceased got burnt to prepare a sketch plan
of the scene of crime. In his deposition,
the I.O. stated that he could not notice any
stove which may have exploded to cause burn
5

--- Page 54 ---
injuries. The sketch plan that he prepared
disclosed that there was a room, hall and
kitchen  besides  other  amenities.  He,
however, did not disclose as to what was
found present in the kitchen, particularly
as to what was the medium of cooking, a gas
stove, or kerosene stove, or an electric
plate/ cooker.
10. The medical papers that were brought
on record are in two sets. The first is
Exhibit P21 which was obtained from M.V.J
Medical  College  and  Research  Hospital,
Dandupalya  where  the  victim  was  taken
immediately  after  she  had  suffered  burn
injuries.  The  other  set  of  papers  were
produced by the defense and was marked as
defense exhibit with the consent / admission
of the prosecution by placing reliance on
the provisions of Section 294 of the Code of
Criminal Procedure, 1973 (for short ‘CrPC’).
11. Exhibit  P21  is  the  document  which
indicates that at about 9:55pm, the victim
was brought to the hospital on 09.06.2010
6

--- Page 55 ---
with burn injuries to the extent of 94%. The
certificate  further  indicates  that  the
victim was bought to the hospital by elder
brother of her husband who was accompanied
by victim’s elder sister, namely, PW1. The
entry in the Wound Certificate prepared at
the hospital reflected that burn injuries
were  suffered  due  to  burst  of  kerosene
stove. The other set of papers which were
produced as defense exhibit and which have
been relied by the High Court while passing
the impugned order would indicate that she
was admitted to the hospital as a case of
accident and that was what she reported to
the Doctor who admitted her in the hospital.
Both injuries certificates indicate that she
had suffered 94% burn injuries.
12. The  Doctor,  who  had  certified  the
medical fitness of the victim for recording
her first dying declaration by the I.O., was
examined as PW17. Extensive suggestions were
made  to  PW17  during  cross-examination
regarding  the  nature  and  extent  burn
7

--- Page 56 ---
injuries  suffered  by  the  victim  and
likelihood of her being put on sedation/
pain killers and, therefore, there being no
possibility  of  her  being  conscious  to
depose. In response to those suggestions,
the Doctor admitted that such patients are
kept on pain killers but claimed that she
was conscious. 
13. However, what is striking here is that
the prosecution has not brought on record
the medical treatment which the victim was
put on while she was alive after having
suffered burn injuries to the extent of 94%.
Those papers would have reflected whether
she was put on sedatives/painkillers and if
so,  to  what  extent.  Interestingly,  the
second set of medical papers, which were
marked as defense exhibit, were produced not
by the prosecution but by the defense and
those were admitted by consent as there was
no challenge to their veracity. In such
circumstances, there was cleavage in the
recordings in the medical papers and the
8

--- Page 57 ---
dying declaration. On the one hand, the
medical papers indicated that the deceased
had suffered burn injuries by an accidental
stove burst and in the dying declaration she
alleged that she was set on fire by her
husband.
14. It  is  well  settled  that  dying
declaration, if wholly reliable, can be the
sole basis of conviction. However, whether a
dying declaration is to be accepted or not,
would  depend  on  the  surrounding
circumstances proven on record. Here the
surrounding circumstances indicated that the
patient was admitted in the Hospital as a
case of a patient who suffered accidental
burns  suffered  from  a  stove  burst.  In
ordinary course such an entry may not have
much weight as against a dying declaration.
However, what is important is that at the
time  of  admission  one  of  the  crucial
prosecution  witnesses,  namely,  PW1,  none
other than the sister of the deceased, was
present. PW1 states that she was informed by
9

--- Page 58 ---
her daughter Savitha about the accident and
on receiving such information she had rushed
to the house of the deceased where she found
the deceased lying down. She also makes a
statement that the deceased disclosed to her
about being set on fire by her husband after
pouring kerosene on her, yet, interestingly,
no FIR comes into existence till 4:30pm of
the next day. If there had been a disclosure
of the nature which PW1 speaks of, there
might not have been a delay in lodging the
FIR. In such circumstances, when we test the
dying  declaration  with  the  surrounding
circumstances of the victim being admitted
in the hospital as a case of accident and,
thereafter, there being a delay of over 18
hours in lodging the FIR, a serious doubt
arises  about  the  veracity  of  the  dying
declaration,  particularly,  when  in  the
medical  papers  prepared  at  the  hospital
where she died, again a case of accidental
burn injuries on account of stove burst is
mentioned.  Besides, the medical treatment
10

--- Page 59 ---
plan to which the deceased was put on while
she was alive has been suppressed. In cases
like this ordinarily a victim would be put
on pain killers/ sedatives to ameliorate the
pain and suffering. As to what would be the
effect of such painkillers or sedatives can
be gauged only when those medical papers are
produced. Here those were not brought on
record despite suggestions to the doctor
during cross examination. 
15. In those circumstances, if the High
Court found that the dying declaration is
not one which could be relied upon, its view
is one of the views possible and, therefore,
sitting in an appeal against acquittal, we
do not find a justifiable reason to reverse
its view. More so, when we notice that the
prosecution  has  withheld  medical  papers
relating to the nature of treatment to which
the victim was put on while she was under
treatment in the hospital where she expired.
16. At this stage, we may consider another
submission raised by the learned counsel for
11

--- Page 60 ---
the appellant. The learned counsel for the
appellant submits that this is a case where
the deceased had died an unnatural death
within 7 years of her marriage and it was
shown that she was harassed in connection
with demand of dowry and, therefore, it was
a  case  where  the  law  would  raise  a
presumption of dowry death under Section
113B of the Indian Evidence Act, 1872.
17. In our view, the presumption would
arise  not  by  merely  proving  that  the
deceased died an unnatural death within 7
years of her marriage but also by proving
that she was subjected to harassment soon
before her death in connection with demand
of dowry2. In the present case, as we have
noticed above, the prosecution evidence is
silent in respect of any demand of dowry
prior to marriage and there is evidence in
respect of the relationship being cordial
for as long as four months after marriage.
The issue appears to be regarding pledging
22 See: Shoor Singh & Another v. State of Uttarakhand, (2025) 2 SCC 815 
12

--- Page 61 ---
of ornaments by the husband. Insofar as the
allegations that there was subsequent demand
of dowry is concerned, those allegations are
not acceptable in absence of any disclosure
about the nature of those demands and as to
when and to whom they were made. Moreover,
the High Court didn’t find any substance in
those  allegations  and,  therefore,  the
accused has been acquitted of the charge of
offence punishable under Section 498A of
IPC. In such circumstances, we do not find a
justifiable  reason  to  raise  presumption
under Section 113B of the Evidence Act for
an offence of dowry death.
18. Now the next question that arises for
our consideration is as to whether we should
acquit  the  respondent  of  the  charge  of
offence punishable under Section 306 IPC
even though he has not preferred an appeal
against his conviction for the said offence.
On this aspect, our attention has been drawn
to a decision of this Court in  Shanti Devi
13

--- Page 62 ---
vs.  State  of  Haryana 3 wherein in paragraph
38, it has been held as follows:
“38. We may, at this stage, note
that  the  appellant-  accused
herein as well as Rajbir (accused
No.2),  being  the  son  of  the
appellant-accused  were  both
provided legal aid counsel in the
appeal  before  the  High  Court,
manifestly  for  the  reason  of
their  poor  financial  status.
Rajbir  (accused  No.2)  has  not
even preferred an appeal against
the judgment of the High Court.
However,  while  discussing  the
case of accused-appellant Shanti
Devi,  we  have  found  that  the
evidence led by the prosecution
is  not  sufficient  and  lacks
credibility  so  as  to  form  a
complete chain of incriminating
circumstances  warranting  the
affirmation  of  the  prosecution
case.  The  entire  case  of  the
prosecution  having  being
discarded, the benefit of this
judgment deserves to be extended
to  the  non-appealing  accused
Rajbir (accused No.2), who has
unfortunately  not  preferred  an
appeal to assail his conviction.
In this regard, we may gainfully
refer  to  Sahadevan  (supra),
wherein this Court observed that:
“Where the Court finds that
the  prosecution  evidence
suffers  from  serious
contradictions,  is
unreliable,  is  ex  facie
neither cogent nor true and
the prosecution has failed
to  discharge  the
33  2025 INSC 987
14

--- Page 63 ---
established onus of proving
the guilt of the accused
beyond  reasonable  doubt,
the  Court  will  be  well
within its jurisdiction to
return  the  finding  of
acquittal and even suo moto
extend  the  benefit  to  a
non-appealing  accused  as
well, more so, where the
Court even disbelieves the
very  occurrence  of  the
crime  itself.  Of  course,
the role attributed to each
of the accused and other
attendant  circumstances
would  be  relevant
considerations  for  the
Court  to  apply  its
discretion  judiciously.
There can be varied reasons
for a non-appealing accused
in  not  approaching  the
appellate  Court.  If,  for
compelling  and  inevitable
reasons, like lack
of finances, absence of any
person to pursue
his  remedy  and  lack  of
proper  assistance  in  the
jail, an accused is unable
to  file  appeal,  then  it
would amount to denial of
access to justice to such
accused.”
  (Emphasis Supplied)
19. Further, in  The  State  of  Assam  vs.
Moinul Haque @ Monu in paragraph 35, it has
been observed as follows:
“35. It is not in dispute that
15

--- Page 64 ---
the accused-respondent has not
preferred  any  appeal
challenging his conviction for
the said offence. However, the
absence of an appeal by the
accused-respondent  does  not,
by itself, denude this Court
of its appellate jurisdiction.
In exercise of powers under
Section  386  of  the  CrPC
[corresponding to Section 427
of  the  Bharatiya  Nagarik
Suraksha Sanhita, 2023], the
appellate Court is vested with
the  power  to  examine  the
correctness  of  the  findings
and sentence recorded by the
Court below and to reverse,
alter or affirm the same, as
the interests of justice may
require.”
20. Having  regard  to  the  aforesaid
decisions and the provisions of Section 386
of CrPC, and taking into consideration that
the dying declaration was justifiably not
accepted  by  the  High  Court  in  the
surrounding circumstances of the case, and
there existed no cogent evidence/material to
convict  the  respondent  for  an  offence
punishable under Section 498-A of IPC, in
our view, there is no good reason to uphold
and sustain the conviction of the respondent
under Section 306 IPC merely because there
16

--- Page 65 ---
was some evidence that the deceased was
unhappy regarding pledge of her ornaments by
the accused. Therefore, in exercise of our
powers under Section 386 of CrPC, we deem it
appropriate to acquit the respondent of the
charge of offence punishable under Section
306 IPC.  If the respondent is still serving
the  sentence  thereunder,  he  shall  be
released.
21. The appeal stands disposed of in the
aforesaid terms.                            
22. Pending application(s), if any, shall
stand disposed of.

New Delhi;

IN THE SUPREME COURT OF INDIA

Umar and Anr.

O R D E R  
 
 Leave granted.  
2. In the above appeal, our attention is to be bestowed on 
the short question as to whether ‘cognizance’ has been taken 
in the protest petition filed under Section 200 of the Code of 
Criminal Procedure, 1973 (Cr.PC ) before or after the 
Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023 (BNSS) came into 
force w.e.f. 01.07.2024. 
3. We need not dwell upon the facts leading to the 
complaint. Suffice it to notice that an FIR was registered on 
07.06.2022, pursuant to which the police filed a Closure 
Report after investigation on 06.08.2022 . On 14.1 0.2022, a  
protest petition was filed in which further investigation was 
ordered under Section 173(8) of the Cr.PC on 29.11.2022. 

--- Page 76 ---
Page 2 of 7 
Crl. A. @SLP (Crl.) No.14343 of 2026 
After further investigation, a Closure Report was again filed 
on 14.02.2023, against which also a protest petition was filed 
on 28.04.2023. The complaint was taken on board on 
11.10.2023 and the same was posted for statement of the 
complainant, which was taken along with that of the other 
witnesses produced, on 06.03.2024.  The matter remained in 
limbo till 03.06.2026 when the learned Special Judge, 
(SC/ST), Azamgarh, by a detailed order prima facie found 
commission of the offence and issued process against the 
respondents.         
4. Mr. Shoeb Alam, learned Senior Counsel appearing 
for the appellant-accused would point out that Annexure P26 
dated 03.06.2026 was the order by which cognizance was 
taken; when the BNSS had come into  force. Necessarily 
cognizance could have been taken only after giving the 
accused an opportunity of being heard as is statutorily 
mandated in the proviso to Section 223 of the BNSS, a 
departure from the pari materia  provision in Cr.PC.   The 
learned Senior Counsel relies on a decision of this Court in 
Parvinder Singh v. Directorate of Enforcement1, to urge two 
 
1 2026 SCC OnLine SC 903 

--- Page 77 ---
Page 3 of 7 
Crl. A. @SLP (Crl.) No.14343 of 2026 
propositions; one, that after the BNSS came into effect, even 
if the crime is registered or committed prior to that, the 
procedure under the BNSS has to be followed, and then, that 
before taking cognizance under the BNSS, the accused has 
to be necessarily heard.   
5. Ms. Srishti Singh,  learned Advocate on Record 
appearing for the respondent-State refutes the contention of 
the appellant, specifically pointing out that cognizance has 
been taken earlier to the BNSS.   
6. Mr. Sayed Waseem Quadri,  learned Senior Counsel 
appearing for the respondent -complainant, would rely on 
two decisions of this Court to buttress h is contention that 
there is no formal order required, to find cognizance having 
been taken and cognizance cannot be confused with the 
issuance of process.  According to him, cogni zance had 
been taken, the minute  the complaint was accepted to the 
files of the Court.  He relies on the decisions in CREF 
Finance Ltd. v. Shree Shanthi Homes (P) Ltd. 2 and Zunaid 
v. State of Uttar Pradesh & Ors.3.   
 
2 (2005) 7 SCC 46 
3 (2023) 14 SCC 576 

--- Page 78 ---
Page 4 of 7 
Crl. A. @SLP (Crl.) No.14343 of 2026 
7.  CREF Finance Ltd.2 was a case in which the record of 
the Magistrate clearly indicated the words ‘cognizance 
taken’ on 01.06.2000. This Court found that even if the same 
was not recorded that would make no difference since 
cognizance taken, is of the offence and not of the offender 
and therefore issuance of process to the offender cannot be 
confused with cognizance.  It was held that once the Court 
on perusal of the complaint is satisfied that it discloses the 
commission of an offence and there is no reason to reject the 
complaint at that stage, and proceeds further in the matter, 
it must be held to have taken cognizance of the offence. (sic 
para 10). 
8. In paragraph 9 of the said judgment, this Court 
referred to a number of decisions to hold that, cognizance is 
used in the Cr.PC to indicate the point when the Magistrate 
or Judge takes judicial notice of the offence and that it was a 
word of indefinite import, depending upon the statutory 
requirement.          
9. Zunaid3 also held that when a police report is received 
under Section 173, Cr.PC, whether it be a Closure Report or 
a chargesheet, the Magistrate has three options; (1) to drop 

--- Page 79 ---
Page 5 of 7 
Crl. A. @SLP (Crl.) No.14343 of 2026 
action, then (2) take cognizance and issue process without 
being bound by the conclusion arrived at by the police and 
lastly, (3) take cognizance of the offence under Section 
190(1)(a) on the basis of the original complaint and proceed 
to examine upon oath the complainant and his witnesses 
under Section 200.  
10. In this context we also have to notice Parvinder Singh1 
which held that under the BNSS taking cognizance is nothing 
but an application of judicial mind and till such judicial mind 
is exercised, an inquiry cannot  be deemed to have  
commenced. This is the position even under the Cr.PC, but 
under the BNSS there is a requirement added to hear the 
accused before exercising the judicial mind. 
11. Essentially, it will depend upon the manner in which 
the Court has proceeded with a report filed by the police, or 
a protest complaint, for which we have to look at the various 
proceedings in the present case. Annexure P23 is the order 
passed on the protest petition, wherein a detailed 
consideration was made and after laying down the options 
available on the submission of a Closure Report, the Court  
found justified an inquiry to be conducted by the Court itself 

--- Page 80 ---
Page 6 of 7 
Crl. A. @SLP (Crl.) No.14343 of 2026 
and directed the complainant to be produced for evidence 
under Section 200 Cr.PC. Hence, the Court did no t take 
cognizance at that stage and required the evidence of the 
complainant which was taken on 06.03.2024. The 
complainant was examined as PW1 , her husband as PW2 
and a Doctor as PW3.  No orders were passed and the matter 
was kept pending for almost two years. On 03.06.2026, 
Annexure P26 order was issued , wherein it was 
categorically found that prima facie  the accused has 
committed the offence s as  alleged and hence process is 
issued to the accused.  
12.  From a reading of the  first order passed, tak ing on 
record the complaint and requiring the complainant’s 
presence for taking evidence , as also the second order 
passed finding prima facie case made out on which process 
was issued, we are of the opinion that it is the second order 
passed on 03.06.2026 that the judicial mind was applied, as 
prima facie case was found, deeming the cognizance having 
been taken. That being so , the Court had to summon the 
accused and hear him before such cognizance was taken.   

--- Page 81 ---
Page 7 of 7 
Crl. A. @SLP (Crl.) No.14343 of 2026 
13. In the above circumstances, we set aside Annexure 
P26 order to the extent ‘prima facie  case was found, and 
cognizance was taken ’ and confine it to a ‘summons 
simplicitor’.  The appellant accused shall appear before the 
Court within a period of one month and on that da y, or on 
any other day if the complainant or her counsel is not 
present, the matter shall be heard and appropriate orders 
passed.    
14. The appeal stands allowed  without any observations 
on the merits of the matter.   
15. Pending application(s), if any, shall stand rejected.   
 
 
……….……………………. J. 
                                               (J. B. PARDIWALA) 
 
………….…………………. J. 
                                                        (K. VINOD CHANDRAN)

SEPTEMBER 23, 2026.

1. I.A.No.  251021  of  2026  –  application  for  intervention/
impleadment is allowed.

Department of Higher Education, Ministry of Education, has filed an

contained in the Note filed by the petitioner have been placed

3. It is further stated in the affidavit that the “ NTA is
transitioning  from  exam-specific  units  to  functional  vertical
structure to build specialized, domain-specific capabilities across
core operational areas.”  It is also stated that “  NTA leadership
has been upgraded with a Secretary level Officer as Director
General, supported by Cross Cadre Officers from IAS, IPS, IRS,

--- Page 84 ---
3
along  with  open-market  recruitment  of  domain  specialists  in

leadership of NTA accordingly now draws officers from IAS, IPS,
IRS, Indian Statistical Service, Indian Ordinance Factory Service,
Indian Supply Service etc. bringing to their specialist expertise
across multiple domains.  Confidential operations have also been
relocated to a dedicated 55,524 sq. ft. government facility at
Minto Road, New Delhi, protected by 39 CISF personnel, X-Ray
screening, metal detectors and strict access control protocols.”
4. The affidavit also suggests various other steps that have been
taken.  We direct NTA to place the affidavit on its web-portal so
that the progress made can be seen by other stakeholders.
5. We direct the Joint Secretary, DoPT, who is coordinating with
the High Powered Task Force to file an affidavit indicating the
progress made by the Committee.  Let the requisite affidavit be
filed within two weeks from today.

IN THE SUPREME COURT OF INDIA
CRIMINAL APPELLATE JURISDICTION
CRIMINAL APPEAL NO.       /2026
  [@ SLP [CRL.] NO. 13505/2026]
SURESH VERMA                      Appellant(s)

STATE OF U.P                    Respondent(s)
WITH
CRIMINAL APPEAL NO.       /2026
[@ SLP(Crl) No. 17210/2026]
O R D E R
Leave granted.
The  appellants  stand  convicted  for  the
offence punishable under Section 3(1) of the U.P.
Gangsters and Anti-Social Activities (Prevention)
Act, 1986 by the Trial Court in SST No.1394/2021
and  have  been  sentenced  to  undergo  rigorous
imprisonment for a period of 10 years.  

--- Page 95 ---
2
By way of the impugned order(s), the High
Court was pleased to dismiss the applications for
suspension of sentence filed by the appellants,
during the pendency of their appeals before the
High Court. 
Aggrieved, the appellants are before this
Court. 
We have heard the learned counsel appearing
for the parties.
Admittedly,  the  appellants  have  already
undergone incarceration for a period of more than
six years and two months approximately and we have
suspended the sentence of other co-convicts as
well. Further, the appeals before the High Court
are of the year 2023, and will take a substantial
time for adjudication.
In such view of the matter, we are inclined
to suspend the sentence imposed on the appellants,
during the pendency of their appeals before the
High Court.

--- Page 96 ---
3
In such view of the matter, the impugned
orders passed by the High Court stand set aside.
The sentence of the appellants stands suspended
and they are granted bail, during the pendency of
their appeals before the High Court, on such terms
and conditions subject to the satisfaction of the
concerned Trial Court.
The appeals stand allowed, accordingly.
Pending application(s), if any, shall stand
disposed of.

[M.M. SUNDRESH]
...................J.

Leave granted.
The  appellant  seeks  anticipatory  bail  in
connection with FIR No.53/2026, dated 26.01.2026,
registered with Police Station - City Sohana,
District – Gurugram, Haryana, for the offences
punishable under Sections 308(2), 351(2) and 64(1)
of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023.  
We have heard the learned counsel appearing
for the parties. 
Learned counsel appearing for the appellants
submits  that  pursuant  to  our  order  dated
07.08.2026,  the  appellant  has  joined  the
investigation  before  the  investigating

--- Page 110 ---
2
authorities.  It  is  a  case  of  a  consensual
relationship turning sour between the parties. The
respondent No.2/complainant despite having been
served, has chosen not to appear before this
Court.  It is further submitted that she is a
married lady, with children.
Learned counsel appearing for the respondent-
State vehemently opposed the grant of anticipatory
bail and submitted that looking to the nature of
the allegations, there is no need for interference
with the impugned order.
Prima facie , we are of the opinion, that it
is a case of a relationship turning sour.  Suffice
it is to state, that the custodial interrogation
of the appellant is not required, particularly,
when the appellant has joined the investigation.
We have also taken note of the fact that despite
being served, the complainant has not chosen to
appear before this Court to oppose the present
appeal.

--- Page 111 ---
3
Considering the aforesaid circumstances, we
are inclined to set aside the impugned order and
grant anticipatory bail to the appellant.
Accordingly, the impugned order stands set
aside and the appellant is granted anticipatory
bail on such terms and conditions subject to the
satisfaction of the concerned Trial Court.
The appeal stands allowed, accordingly.
Pending application(s), if any, shall stand
disposed of.

2026 INSC 1059
 
Page 1 of 5 
CA @ SLP(C) No.28834 of 2026 
 
 
Non-Reportable 
 
 
IN THE SUPREME COURT OF INDIA 
CIVIL APPELLATE JURISDICTION 
 
Civil Appeal No.                 of 2026 
[ @ Special Leave Petition (C) No.28834 of 2026 ] 
 
 
 
Indian Institute of Management 
Jammu                 ...Appellant 
 
 
Versus 
 
 
M/s Ramacivil India Construction 
Pvt. Ltd. & Anr.            ...Respondents 
 
O R D E R 
 
Leave granted. 
2. The matter arises from the impugned order, which set 
aside the impleadment of the appellant herein and 
constituted an Arbitra l Tribunal to resolve the disputes 
between respondent Nos. 1 and 2. The appellant does not 
challenge the constitution of the Arbitral Tribunal but is only 
concerned with the rejection of its prayer for impleadment, 
since the arbitration  was with respect to a construction 
project in its premises, which was entrusted to CPWD, the 

--- Page 120 ---
 
Page 2 of 5 
CA @ SLP(C) No.28834 of 2026 
 
 
second respondent, who in turn entrusted the same to the 
first respondent. 
3. The first respondent was declared successful in a bid 
by the CPWD for the work of construction of Permanent 
campus under Phase-1 for the appellant , and a Letter of 
Acceptance dated 16.10.2020 was issued by the CPWD . In 
the course of execution of the project, certain disputes arose 
between respondent Nos.1 and 2 , and the matter reached 
the High Court in an application under Section 11  of the 
Arbitration and Conciliation Act, 1996 1. There were also 
applications filed under Section 9 of the Act , all of which 
were clubbed together and heard along with a Chamber 
Appeal with respect to the impleadment of the appellant 
herein. 
4. The learned Single Judge disagreed with the findings 
of the Joint Registrar that the appellant had a ‘positive, direct 
and substantial involvement’ in the subject matter sought to 
be referred for resolution by arbitration. It was held that 
 
1 For brevity, ‘the Act’ 

--- Page 121 ---
 
Page 3 of 5 
CA @ SLP(C) No.28834 of 2026 
 
 
large infrastructure projects often  involved several 
institutional stakeholders at different levels and if every  
beneficiary were regarded as a necessary party , arbitral 
proceedings would cease to retain their consensual 
character and instead be converted into sprawling multi -
party disputes detached from the contractual foundation of 
arbitration law. 
5. We are of the opinion that this is not a case in which 
the appellant is said to be  one of the stakeholders; 
especially when the work was with respect to the 
construction project for the benefit of the appellant carried 
out in its premises. The construction was entrusted with the 
CPWD, which had carried out the auction in which the first 
respondent turned out successful. It w as also clear that , in 
addition to the supervision of work by the CPWD Engineers, 
the representatives of the appellant were empowered with 
regular and periodic inspections of the ongoing activities, 
and the deficiencies and shortcomings could be pointed out 
to the CPWD Engineers. We pointed out to the CPWD 

--- Page 122 ---
 
Page 4 of 5 
CA @ SLP(C) No.28834 of 2026 
 
 
Engineers for the purpose of cautioning the contractor, who 
in turn intimated. 
6. In the above circumstances, we are of the opinion that 
though the agreement was between the CPWD and a 
contractor, the appellant herein  is a veritable party to the 
dispute and should be participated ; especially since 
eventually the liability may fall on the appellant. 
7. In the above circumstances, we are of the opinion that 
the appellant should participate in the arbitration initiated 
by the impugned order. The appellant, though cannot raise 
any independent claims, would be entitled to dispute the 
claims/contentions raised by either of the parties and 
apprise the Arbitration Tribunal of the ground realities and 
the factual situation from time to time, in the execution of the 
contract and its closure. 
8. We make it clear that the participation of the appellant 
cannot result in any particular claim being raised by the 
appellant, since any such claim would have to be 
adjudicated as between the appellant and the CPWD ; the 
appellant’s role would be essentially in assisting the 

--- Page 123 ---
 
Page 5 of 5 
CA @ SLP(C) No.28834 of 2026 
 
 
Arbitral Tribunal to come to a just decision while resolving 
the dispute between the respondent Nos.1 and 2; the 
contractor and the CPWD, the result of which could 
substantially impact the appellant also. 
9. With the above observations, the appeal stands 
allowed. 
10. Pending application(s), if any, shall stand rejected. 
 
 
.......…….……………………. J. 
                (J. B. PARDIWALA) 
 
 
....……….……………………. J. 
                            (K. VINOD CHANDRAN) 
 
NEW DELHI; 
SEPTEMBER 24, 2026.

        UPON hearing the counsel the Court made 
the following
                      O R D E R
1. The  petitioner  who  has  been  in  judicial
custody for over seven months since 27.01.2026 in
connection with FIR No.  11993010260023 of 2026
registered  at P.S Rapar, District  Kachchh East
under  Section  103(1)  of  the  Bharatiya  Nyaya
Sanhita, 2023 and Section 135 of the Gujarat
Police Act, 1951, is seeking regular bail.
2. It is alleged that the Petitioner, aged 24
years has stabbed the deceased – brother of the
complainant on the lower left abdomen, resulting
in his death.
3. The  learned  Counsel  for  the  petitioner
submitted that the FIR was not filed on the night
of the incident; and that the deceased declined
hospital treatment on that night, although he was
allegedly stabbed in the abdomen and “blood was
oozing”; he went to the hospital the next day
where  he  succumbed  to  death.  It  was  further
SLP (Crl.) No.16226/2026 2

--- Page 135 ---
submitted that the Petitioner was not named in the
first Wardi given to the doctor by the complainant
at the time of the deceased’s admission to the
hospital, the Petitioner’s name surfaced for the
first time in a subsequent Wardi recorded by the
same doctor later on the same day. Further, the
weapon recovered at the instance of the Petitioner
did not contain any blood stains. It is submitted
that chargesheet has been filed, and that the
Petitioner has no criminal antecedents.
4. On  a  prima  facie  consideration  of  the
material on record and after hearing the learned
counsel for the parties, we are of the opinion
that the petitioner can be released on bail, on
such terms and conditions that the Ld. Additional
Sessions  Judge,  Kachchh  at  Bhachau  (the
jurisdictional court) may deem fit and appropriate
to impose. It is ordered accordingly. 
5. The petitioner shall appear before the Ld.
Additional Sessions Judge, Kachchh at Bhachau as
and when directed and cooperate with the trial.
SLP (Crl.) No.16226/2026 3

--- Page 136 ---
6. With the above directions, the Special Leave
Petition is disposed of.
7. Pending application(s), if any, shall also
stand disposed of.

IN THE SUPREME COURT OF INDIA
CIVIL APPELLATE JURISDICTION
  
    CIVIL APPEAL NO.         OF 2026
    (Arising out of SLP(C) No.34888 of 2025)  
    
DAYA BAI MAGHVANI & ORS.                         APPELLANTS
A1 : DAYA BAI MAGHVANI
A2 : NARESH KUMAR MAGHVANI
A3 : KUMARI REETU MAGHVANI
A4 : KUMARI YASHIKA MAGHVANI
                                VERSUS
THE NEW INDIA ASSURANCE COMPANY LIMITED & ORS.          RESPONDENTS
R1 : THE NEW INDIA ASSURANCE COMPANY LIMITED 
R2 : HARIOM BHARTI
R3 : INDERMANI MINERAL INDIA PVT. LTD.
O R D E R
Heard learned counsel for the petitioners and learned counsel
for the respondent no.1-New Indian Assurance Company Limited.
2. Leave granted.
3. The present appeal arises out of the impugned judgment dated
21.08.2025 passed by the High Court of Chhattisgarh at Bilaspur in
MAC No.808 of 2024 by which, the amount of compensation of

--- Page 150 ---
2
Rs.62,04,430/- (Rupees Sixty Two Lakhs Four Thousand Four Hundred
Thirty) along with interest @9% per annum as awarded to the
appellants/claimants by the Sixth Additional Motor Accident Claims
Tribunal, Bilaspur, Chhattisgarh (for short, the “MACT”) vide Award
dated 08.01.2024 in Claim Case No.150 of 2022 on the ground of
death of Rupesh Maghwani in a motor accident, has been reduced to
Rs.44,80,474/-  (Rupees  Forty  Four  Lakhs  Eighty  Thousand  Four
Hundred Seventy Four) and the rate of interest has also been
reduced from 9% per annum to 8% per annum.
4. Learned counsel for the appellants submitted that the order of
the MACT did not require any reduction in the compensation awarded
by it and the High Court has erred in doing the same. It is further
submitted that the MACT had carefully gone into the evidence on
record and in fact on certain issues has also taken a view against
appellants, especially with regard to the income of the deceased.
Moreover, it was submitted that the claimants had in fact a good
case for enhancement of the amount rather than reducing the same
substantially as has been done by the High Court.
5. Learned counsel for the respondent no.1-New India Assurance
Company Limited submitted that the High Court has rightly accepted
the contention of the Insurance Company with regard to the actual
loss which the appellants had suffered and that the reduction made
was correct, being based on relevant/sustainable grounds which did
not require any interference by this Court. 
6. We find that the above Award of the MACT is well considered
and in matters like the present, the view taken by the MACT cannot
be said to be unreasonable or perverse. Thus, the reduction in the

--- Page 151 ---
3
amount of compensation awarded by the MACT by the High Court
appears to be not proper.
7. Accordingly, the appeal is partly allowed by restoring the
amount of compensation as awarded by the MACT i.e., Rs.62,04,430/-
(Rupees  Sixty  Two  Lakhs  Four  Thousand  Four  Hundred  Thirty).
However, we feel that the rate of interest as awarded by the High
Court of 8% per annum is reasonable.
8. In view of the above, let an amount of Rs.62,04,430/- (Rupees
Sixty Two Lakhs Four Thousand Four Hundred Thirty) along with
interest @8% per annum from the date of filing of the claim
petition  be  paid  to  the  appellants/claimants  after  adjusting
whatever amount which may have been paid during the interregnum.
The same be done within six weeks from today.
9. Pending application(s), if any, shall stand disposed of.

CRIMINAL APPEAL  NO  .                 OF 2026
(@ SLP(CRL.) No.14974/2026)
SUDHANSU SEKHAR ROUT                  APPELLANT(S)
                           VERSUS
  STATE OF ODISHA                      RESPONDENT(S)
O R D E R
1. Leave granted.
2. We  have  heard  the  learned  Senior  Counsel
appearing for the appellant, and the learned
counsel appearing for the respondent-State. 
3. The  appellant  apprehends  his  arrest  in
connection with FIR No.561 of 2026, registered
at Capital Police Station – Bhubaneswar, for the
offences punishable under Sections 353, 196 and
356 of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023 (for
short, ‘the BNS’).
4. Learned  Senior  counsel  appearing  for  the
appellant  submitted  that  prima  facie  charge
under Section 196 of the BNS is not made out,
and in that eventuality, the remaining charges
would not warrant custodial interrogation of the
1 SLP(Crl.) No.14974/2026

--- Page 158 ---
2
appellant.  The  antecedents  in  which  the
appellant is stated to be involved are not
relevant  insofar  as  the  present  case  is
concerned. The appellant has also joined the
investigation.
5. The  learned  counsel  appearing  for  the
respondent-State substantially relied upon the
counter  affidavit  to  submit  that  there  are
numerous  criminal  antecedents  in  which  the
appellant has been involved. The learned counsel
also placed reliance upon the statements made by
the witnesses in support of the said submission.
6. Upon  hearing  the  learned  Senior  counsel
appearing for the appellant and the learned
counsel appearing for the State, and after going
through the allegations made in the FIR, it is
suffice to state that the appellant is entitled
for anticipatory bail, as on a prima facie view,
the charge under Section 196 of the BNS, may not
be made out. 
7. We are conscious in making the said observation
while making it clear that our observations will
not have any bearing in the further proceedings
before the Trial Court and, therefore, is only
confined to the present appeal. 
8. In  such  view  of  the  matter,  the  remaining
2 SLP(Crl.) No.14974/2026

--- Page 159 ---
3
charges levelled against the appellant would not
warrant the custodial interrogation. The fact
that the appellant is involved in numerous other
criminal cases cannot be a ground to decline the
relief of anticipatory bail. 
9. In such view of the matter, we are inclined to
set aside the impugned order passed by the High
Court,  and  grant  anticipatory  bail  to  the
appellant.
10. Accordingly,  the  impugned  order  stands  set
aside, and the appellant is granted anticipatory
bail, subject to the terms and conditions that
may be imposed by the concerned Trial Court.
11. Needless  to  state  that  the  appellant  shall
cooperate with the pending investigation.
12. The appeal stands allowed, accordingly.
13. Pending  application(s),  if  any,  shall  stand
disposed of.

1. Leave granted.

learned counsel appearing for the respondent-
State.

4. The appellant has been arraigned as an accused
and seeks regular bail in connection with FIR
No.1989/2021, registered at Police Station –
Palakkad Town South, District – Palakkad for the
offences punishable under Sections 143, 144,
147, 148, 341, 302, 120B, 201, 212, 109, 118,

5. The appellant has been under incarceration for
1 SLP(Crl.) No.12492/2026

--- Page 162 ---
approximately five years and material witnesses
have  been  examined  before  the  Trial  Court
including the eye-witnesses. Though the learned
counsel  appearing  for  the  respondent-State
vehemently  opposed  the  prayer  for  bail,
particularly,  on  the  ground  that  there  are
attempts made to influence the witnesses, we
find that more than a year has elapsed even
thereafter. As of now, the material witnesses
have been examined, including the eye-witnesses.
6. Taking note of the long period of incarceration
and  the  factum  of  examination  of  the  eye-
witnesses, we are inclined to set aside the
impugned order passed by the High Court, and
grant regular bail to the appellant.
7. Accordingly,  the  impugned  order  stands  set
aside, and the appellant is granted regular
bail, subject to the terms and conditions that
may be imposed by the concerned Trial Court,
including the condition to make sure that there
will  not  be  any  attempt  to  influence  the
remaining witnesses who are yet to be examined.
8. The Trial Court shall, additionally, impose a
condition that the appellant shall cooperate
2 SLP(Crl.) No.12492/2026

--- Page 163 ---
with the pending trial, particularly when the
trial is scheduled to continue from 01.10.2026.
9. The appeal stands allowed, accordingly.

disposed of.

IN THE SUPREME COURT OF INDIA
CRIMINAL APPELLATE JURISDICTION
CRIMINAL APPEAL NO.           OF 2026  
(@ SPECIAL LEAVE PETITION (CRIMINAL) NO. 8199/2026)
KAKKIRALA SRIHARI                                       Appellant(s)
                                VERSUS
RAMPALLI SRINIVAS & ANR.                               Respondent(s)
ORDER
1.   We have heard learned counsel for the parties.
2. Leave granted.
3. The appellant is facing two proceedings under Section 138 of
the  Negotiable  Instruments  Act,  1881:  one  before  the  XII
Metropolitan Magistrate at Hyderabad being STC NI No. 1172/2023 and
the other is STC NI No. 119/2023 on the file of the learned Judicial
Magistrate First Class (Special Mobile), Medak, Telangana. In both
proceedings, the parties are the same.
4. In such circumstances, the appellant moved a Transfer Petition
before the jurisdictional High Court i.e. the High Court for the
State  of  Telangana  at  Hyderabad  ( briefly,  the  ‘High  Court’
hereinafter) for transfer of the second case i.e. STC NI No.
119/2023 from the file of learned Judicial Magistrate First Class
(Special Mobile), Medak to the file of learned XII Metropolitan
Magistrate at Hyderabad so that both the cases can be tried
together.
5. By the order dated  18.12.2025, a learned Single Judge of the
High Court dismissed the Transfer Petition holding that it did not
merit any consideration.

--- Page 170 ---
6. We find that Section 142A of the Negotiable Instruments Act,
1881 was inserted in the statute vide an amendment in the year 2015.
Sub-section (2) of Section 142A is relevant and is extracted
hereinunder:
“(2) Notwithstanding anything contained in sub-section (2)
of section 142 or sub sect ion (1), where the payee or the
holder in due course, as the case may be, has filed a
complaint against the drawer of a cheque in the court
having jurisdiction under sub-section (2) of section 142 or
the case has been transferred to that court under sub-
section (1) and such complaint is pending in that court,
all  subsequent  complaints  arising  out  of  section  138
against the same drawer shall  be filed before the same
court irrespective of whether those cheques were delivered
for  collection  or  presented  for  payment  within  the
territorial jurisdiction of that court.”
7. From the above, we find that there is a statutory mandate that
all  subsequent  complaints  arising  out  of  Section  138  of  the
Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881 against the same drawer shall be
filed before the same Court where a previous complaint is pending
against the same drawer irrespective of whether those cheques were
delivered  for  collection  or  presented  for  payment  within  the
territorial jurisdiction of that court.
8. Learned  Single  Judge,  while  rejecting  the  prayer  of  the
appellant, failed to take note of the aforesaid statutory provision.
9. That being the position, we set aside the impugned order of the

--- Page 171 ---
High Court dated 18.12.2025 and transfer STC NI No. 119/2023 on the
file  of  the  learned  Judicial  Magistrate  First  Class  (Special
Mobile), Medak to the file of learned XII Metropolitan Magistrate at
Hyderabad and to be adjudicated along with STC NI No.1172/2023
already pending on the file of the XII Metropolitan Magistrate at
Hyderabad.
13.   Accordingly, the Criminal Appeal is allowed.
14.   Pending application(s), if any, shall stand disposed of.
…………………………………………………...J.
              [UJJAL BHUYAN]
…………………………………………………...J.
      [ATUL S. CHANDURKAR]
NEW DELHI;
23rd SEPTEMBER, 2026.

--- Page 172 ---

WITH

IN THE SUPREME COURT OF INDIA
CIVIL APPELLATE JURISDICTION
CIVIL APPEAL NO(s).      OF 2026
(@ SPECIAL LEAVE PETITION (CIVIL) NO(s).12016/2026)
VARUN GUPTA AND CO.                         APPELLANT(S)
VERSUS
UNION OF INDIA & ANR.                       RESPONDENT(S)
O R D E R
1. Leave granted.
2. Heard Mr. Ganesh Khanna, learned counsel appearing
for  the  appellant,  and  Mr.  Anil  Kaushik,  learned
Additional Solicitor General appearing for the Union of
India.
3. By the impugned order, the High Court has appointed an
arbitrator from CPWD. The appellant is aggrieved because
CPWD is the department in question and the individual
appointed by the High Court was employed with CPWD, which
is contrary to decisions of this Court.
4. When the matter came up before us, we put it to Mr.
Anil Kaushik, learned Additional Solicitor General, as to
whether  any  other  independent  arbitrator  who  is  not
involved with CPWD could be considered and a consensus
arrived at.
5. Today, the parties have jointly reported that all
disputes between the parties would be referred to Major
General K. Gajria, ADGTE, Army (Corps of Engineers).
1

--- Page 182 ---
6. In view of the above, in supersession of the order of
the High Court, we direct as follows:
(i) Major General K. Gajria, ADGTE, Army (Corps of
Engineers),  is  appointed  as  sole  arbitrator  to
arbitrate the dispute.
(ii) The parties shall get in touch with the learned
Arbitrator.
(iii) The fees of the learned Arbitrator shall be fixed
in terms of the Fourth Schedule to the Arbitration and
Conciliation Act, 1996.
(iv) Learned  counsel  appearing  for  the  appellant
submits that the statement of claim has already been
filed before the erstwhile Arbitrator. Their claim and
other documents shall be transferred to the newly
appointed Arbitrator.
7. The impugned order dated 17.10.2025 shall stand set
aside. The appeal stands disposed of in the above terms.
8. Pending application(s), if any, shall stand disposed
of.
......................J.
   (K.V. VISWANATHAN)
......................J.
    (ARUN PALLI)
NEW DELHI
23-09-2026

ITEM NO.26               COURT NO.6               SECTION II
               S U P R E M E  C O U R T  O F  I N D I A
                       RECORD OF PROCEEDINGS
SPECIAL LEAVE PETITION (CRIMINAL) Diary No.51725/2026
[Arising  out  of  impugned  final  judgment  and  order  dated
22-05-2026 in CRLA No. 162/2025 passed by the Gauhati High
Court]
MD. ZAHANGIR ALAM                                  Petitioner(s)
                                VERSUS
THE STATE OF ASSAM & ANR.                          Respondent(s)
IA No. 284473/2026 - CONDONATION OF DELAY IN FILING
 
Date : 25-09-2026 This matter was called on for hearing today.
CORAM : 
         HON'BLE MR. JUSTICE J.B. PARDIWALA
         HON'BLE MR. JUSTICE K. VINOD CHANDRAN
For Petitioner(s) :Mr. Parthiv K. Goswami, Sr. Adv.
                   Mr. Rahul Pratap, AOR
                   Ms. Atiga Singh, Adv.
                   Ms. Lakshmi Agarwal, Adv.
                   Mr. Abhishek Jaiswal, Adv.
                   Mr. Muhammad Maroof, Adv.
                   
                   
For Respondent(s) : 
Diary No.51725/2026                                                         1

--- Page 190 ---
          UPON hearing the counsel the Court made the following
                             O R D E R
1. Delay condoned.
2. Leave granted.
3. The appellant who has been convicted and sentenced to 20
years of rigorous imprisonment through concurrent findings of
the Trial Court and the High Court for offences under Section
363 Indian Penal Code, 1860 (for short, “IPC”) and Section 6 of
Protection of Children from Sexual Offences Act, 2012 (for
short, “POCSO Act”) in connection with FIR No. 9 of 2024
registered at P.S. Kajalgaon, District Chirang, seeks suspension
of the sentence imposed on him, and release on bail, pending the
final disposal of his appeal against conviction before this
Court.
4. It is alleged that the appellant, aged 19 years seduced the
prosecutrix, aged 17 years into a relationship, and engaged in
sexual intercourse with her, recorded her videos, and when she
refused to engage in sexual intercourse with him again, shared
it with his friends, and then online.
5. The Trial Court convicted the appellant holding that the
prosecution has proved the allegations against the appellant
Diary No.51725/2026                                                         2

--- Page 191 ---
through the evidence of the prosecutrix, evidence substantiating
circulation of intimate videos online, and relying on her birth
certificate to ascertain her as a minor at the time of incident
in  2023.  The  Trial  Court  imposed  a  sentence  of  rigorous
imprisonment of 20 years with fine of Rs. 50,000/- under section
6 of POCSO Act; and rigorous imprisonment of 2 years with fine
of Rs. 50,000/- for the offence under Section 363 of the IPC.
6. The High Court vide the impugned order upheld the order of
conviction and sentence holding that the prosecutrix is a
sterling  witness;  and  that  medical  evidence  need  not  be
considered for ascertainment of age, when school certificate is
available.
7. The learned Counsel for the appellant submitted that the
appellant himself was an adolescent, aged 19 years at the time
of the alleged incident and has already undergone a period of 1
year 7 months in custody. It was stated that the prosecutrix in
her cross-examination admitted to being in a consensual and
ongoing relationship with the appellant for approximately 8 to 9
months prior to the alleged incident. It was further submitted
that PW 10, the Health Officer who medically examined the
victim, in her cross-examination stated that based on the
Diary No.51725/2026                                                         3

--- Page 192 ---
radiological/X-ray  examination,  the  age  of  the  victim  was
estimated to be between 19 and 21 years. There is also a delay
of six months between the date of occurrence alleged in the FIR,
i.e., 27.07.2023 and the date of filing of FIR on 27.01.2024.
8. Considering the doubt insofar as the age determination of
the prosecutrix, the delay in filing the FIR, and the age of the
accused Petitioner, we are of the view that the sentence imposed
on the appellant can be suspended.
9. In view of the aforesaid, we suspend the substantive order
of sentence of rigorous imprisonment for 20 years imposed on the
appellant and order that the appellant be released on bail
forthwith, if not required in any other case, subject to terms
and conditions that the Court of Special Judge, Chirang at
Kajalgaon, may deem fit to impose.
10. The Registry shall call for records from the Trial Court,
and soft copy of the records shall be supplied to the Ld.
Counsel appearing for the appellant and the State.
  (CHANDRESH)                                  (POOJA SHARMA)
 ASTT. REGISTRAR-cum-PS                      COURT MASTER (NSH)
Diary No.51725/2026                                                         4

--- Page 193 ---
REVISED

SLP (C) No(s).33394-33395/2026 (It.23)
1. We have heard learned Senior Counsel for the petitioner at a
considerable  length.  Learned  counsel  for  the  contesting
respondents, who are on caveat, have also been heard.
2. We see no ground to interfere with the impugned judgment of
the High Court. All that we find necessary to clarify is that the
observations  made  by  the  High  Court  in  various  paragraphs,
particularly paragraphs 201, 202, 213, 219, 220, 222, 223, 225,
227, 237, 241, 243, 244, and 252, are tentative and only for the
purpose of making out a case for forensic audit.
3. It goes without saying that the forensic audit shall be
conducted independently, without being influenced by the above-
mentioned observations.
4. The Special Leave Petitions are, accordingly, dismissed.
5. Pending applications, if any, also stand disposed of.

                   Mr. Sinha Shrey Nikhilesh, Adv.
                   Mr. Parth Verma, Adv.              
          UPON hearing the counsel the Court made the following
                             O R D E R
1. The  instant  writ  petition,  purportedly  filed  in  public
interest under Article 32 of the Constitution of India, seeks a
direction to various Investigating Agencies/Statutory Authorities
to investigate the alleged ARC-facilitated banking fraud concerning
JKM Infra Projects Limited.
2. At the very outset, the learned senior counsel appearing for
the contesting respondents, in unanimity, raised objections to the
maintainability of such a PIL, alleging that the same has been
filed at the instance of respondent No. 18. Therefore, vide our
order dated 07.09.2026, the petitioners were directed to file an
affidavit disclosing their complete particulars and bona fides , as
well as the source of the information and documents basis which the
instant petition had been instituted.
3. In compliance thereto, the petitioners have filed a joint
affidavit furnishing the information sought by this Court.
4. We have perused the affidavit. 
5. Keeping in view the nature of proceedings and the material
placed on record, we are not inclined to entertain this PIL.
However, we hasten to clarify that our decision to not entertain
the petition shall not be construed as casting any aspersion on the
bona fides of the petitioners, who are young members of the Bar. We
have no doubt that in future, with more experience at the Bar, they
will have ample opportunities to raise issues of genuine public
3

--- Page 204 ---
importance through appropriate proceedings.
6. Consequently, the instant writ petition is dismissed.
7. At the same time, we deem it necessary to clarify that the
non-entertainment of the instant writ petition will not preclude
(i) Directorate of Enforcement; (ii) Serious Fraud Investigation
Office, (iii) Central Bureau of Investigation; and (iv) Reserve
Bank of India from taking cognizance of the allegations concerning
the aforesaid alleged banking fraud. Any public-spirited person
shall be at liberty to assist these agencies in accordance with
law.
8. It is further clarified that we have not expressed any opinion
on the merits of the matter.
9. Pending applications, if any, also stand disposed of.
(ARJUN BISHT)                              (PREETHI DILEEP KUMAR)

Date : 25-09-2026 This matter was called on for hearing today.

                   Mr. Parth Kaushik, Adv.

For Respondent(s) : 
          UPON hearing the counsel the Court made the following
                             O R D E R
1. A Single Judge of the High Court while disposing of the petitioners’ writ
petition vide order dated 22nd December, 2025 had required the Ministry of Civil
Aviation, Government of India to consider and decide the petitioners’ grievance
in accordance with law.
2. However, upon applications being filed after disposal of the writ petition,
direction was given to the Director General of Civil Aviation 1 to consider the
grievance of the petitioners.
3. The order was carried in appeal and the Division Bench of the High Court
1 DGCA

--- Page 218 ---
2
while declining interference on the ground of availability of a remedy before the
DGCA has disposed of the appeal by the impugned order dated 23rd May, 2026. 
4. Prior to the special leave petition being filed in August, 2026 challenging
the appellate order dated 23 rd May, 2026, the DGCA has declined to entertain
the petitioners’ grievance and has rejected it vide an order dated 6th July, 2026.
5. It is in the aforesaid factual backdrop that we are called upon to consider
whether the High Court was right in subsequently requiring the DGCA to look
into the petitioners’ grievance.
6. Insofar as the point taken by the petitioners with regard to alteration of
the identity of the authority who was to consider their grievance is concerned,
we accept that the High Court ought not to have made such alteration after the
writ petition stood disposed of.
7. Be that as it may, having regard to the fact that the order dated 6 th July,
2026 has now been passed by the DGCA and that an appeal lies in terms of
provisions contained in Rule 3-B of the Aircraft Rules, 1937 against an order of
the DGCA to the Ministry of Civil Aviation, Government of India, we grant liberty
to the petitioners to challenge the order of the DGCA before the Principal
Secretary to the Government of India, Ministry of Civil Aviation within a period
of 30 days from date.  Once such appeal is filed, the same shall be decided in
accordance with law upon granting due opportunity of hearing to the petitioners
as well as the respondents. The appeal shall also be disposed of by passing a
reasoned order.
8. All points / contentions, on merits, are kept open.
9. The Ministry shall decide the appeal without being influenced by any
observation in the order of the Single Judge or the Division Bench of the High
Court, as expeditiously as possible.

--- Page 219 ---
3
10. The special leave petition is disposed of in the above terms.
11. Pending interlocutory application(s), if any, shall also stand disposed of.
(JATINDER KAUR)                              (SUDHIR KUMAR SHARMA)

--- Page 232 ---
IN THE SUPREME COURT OF INDIA
CIVIL APPELLATE JURISDICTION
CIVIL APPEAL NO……………..OF 2026
(@Special Leave Petition (Civil) No.5179 of 2020)
THE ALARANATH DHANDA MULAKA 
MAHAVIDYALAYA THROUGH 
SECRETARY CUM PRINCIPAL                                         … APPELLANT(S)
VERSUS
LAXMIDHARA SAMANTASINGHARA (DEAD)
THR. LRS. & ORS.                                                             …RESPONDENT(S)
O R D E R
1. Leave granted.
2. On 14 th of October 1978, a gift deed of an immovable property was
executed in favour of the present appellant – original Defendant No.1, by
Defendant No.2 – the father of the plaintiffs.
3. On the said land, Defendant No.1 set up a college which has been
functional since September 1981.  Sometime in the year 1989, the plaintiffs,
sons of Defendant No.2, filed a suit seeking a declaration qua the said gift deed
to be illegal and void on the premise that the suit land was not the individual
property of Defendant No.2, but joint family property in which the plaintiffs
also had an interest.  The suit was decreed  ex  parte  vide  judgment dated
01.07.2000.  Significantly, the plaintiffs sat over the decree till the year 2016

--- Page 233 ---
i.e., 15 years and thereafter only filed an application for execution.  It is at this
point in time, the donee i.e., Defendant No.1 learnt about the decree ex parte in
nature, to have been passed with respect to the suit land.  Resultantly, Defendant
No.1 took steps in accordance with law. 
 
4. The District and Sessions Judge vide judgment dated 08.02.2018 in RFA
No.31 of 2016 set aside the said ex parte decree by condoning the delay of 15
years and 7 months.  However, the High Court vide order dated 18.09.2019 in
CRP No.4 of 2018 set aside the same, for the Defendant No.1 did not furnish
sufficient explanation on the issue of delay.  It is in the aforesaid backdrop we
are called upon to decide the present appeal.
5.      It is a matter of record that certain facts are undisputed.  The suit property
was gifted in the year 1978 by Defendant No.2 in favour of Defendant No.1.
Soon thereafter, i.e., from 21.09.1981, an educational institution was set up
which became functional.  It was only in the year 1989 that a suit was filed,
challenging the said gift deed, in which Defendant No.1 was proceeded  ex
parte.     Defendant No.1 is in possession the possession of the property.
Defendant  No.2  even  though  associated  with  Defendant  No.1,  never  ever
informed him/Defendant No.1 of the passing of decree.  The execution of the
decree was put on hold for over 15 years. 
 
6.     Having heard learned counsel for the parties, we are of the considered view
that the High Court committed a grave error in entertaining the revision petition
and setting aside the order dated 08.02.2018 passed by the District Judge, Puri,
who  allowed  the  application  under  Section  5  of  the  Limitation  Act  by
entertaining the regular first appeal.  It is argued that the decree which is
collusive in nature, was only obtained to defeat the right of a party, more so on
account of the prices of the land having risen considerably.  While we refrain

--- Page 234 ---
from commenting about the conduct of either the plaintiff or their father i.e.,
Defendant No.2, but considering the overall conspectus, we are inclined to
interfere with the impugned order, for Defendant No.2  did furnish sufficient
explanation in taking recourse to such steps as were permissible in law, in
getting an ex parte decree set aside.  
7. It is argued that the suit was not maintainable in view of the applicable
municipal law(s).  We are not making any observation on the same and leave
the issue open for the same to be considered during trial.  The appeal is allowed.
The  ex parte  decree dated 01.07.2000 in T.S.No.159/1989  titled  Sri Kapila
Samantisinghar & ors.  vs. The Alarnath Dhanda Mulaka Mahavidyalaya,
Managing Committee represented through its Secretary and Anr. , is quashed
and set aside.  The suit is restored to its original number for it to be tried on
expeditious basis in accordance with law.  Parties to appear before the Trial
Court on 08.09.2026.  Pending applications(s) if any shall stand disposed of.
……………………….…………………….J.
(SANJAY KAROL)
………………………………………….…..J.
(NONGMEIKAPAM KOTISWAR SINGH)
New Delhi;
August 3, 2026

--- Page 235 ---
1

PRASHANT K MEHTA & ANR.                            Petitioner(s)

IA No. 277684/2026 - ADDITION / DELETION / MODIFICATION  PARTIES

DOCUMENTS/FACTS/ANNEXURES
 
Date : 23-09-2026 This matter was called on for hearing today.
CORAM : 
         HON'BLE MR. JUSTICE M.M. SUNDRESH
         HON'BLE  MR. JUSTICE PRASANNA B. VARALE
For Petitioner(s) Mr. Senthil Jagadeesan, Sr. Adv.
                  Mr. Tarun Mehra, Adv.
                   Mr. Ankur Saigal, Adv.
                   Ms. Jyoti Ghag, Adv.
                   Mr. Abhishek Prabhu, Adv.
                   Ms. Anushree Kapooria, Adv.
                   Mr. Mohd. Naved, Adv.
                   Mr. Ankit Singhal, Adv.
                   Mr. Shivendra Singh, AOR
                   
For Respondent(s) Mr. Zoeb Cuttelerywala, Adv.
                   Ms. Vikram Kamath, Adv.
                   Mr. Sameer Sharma, Adv.
                   Mr. Advait Shukla, Adv.
                   Mr. Ankit Kumar Sinha, Adv.
                   Mr. Hasan Murtaza, AOR
                   
          UPON hearing the counsel the Court made the following
                             O R D E R
We  have  heard  the  learned  Senior  counsel
appearing  for  the  petitioners  and  the  learned
counsel appearing for the respondent.

--- Page 236 ---
2
Learned  Senior  counsel  appearing  for  the
petitioners would submit that the condition imposed
on the petitioners to pay costs amounting to a sum
of Rs.5 lakh to the  respondent No.2- HDFC Bank Ltd.
and a sum of Rs.5 lakh to the Armed Forces Battle
Casualties Welfare Fund is excessive, apart from the
fact, that a factually wrong recording has been made
on the question of delay.
Learned counsel appearing for the respondent
submits that there is no need for interference with
the impugned order, particularly, by taking note of
the fact that the complaint was given over two
decades ago.
Upon hearing the learned counsel appearing for
the parties, we are not inclined to go into the
submissions made on merits, with respect to the
statement made on behalf of the petitioners on the
issue of delay as to whether it was rightly made or
false.
Suffice it is to state, that the condition
imposed on the petitioners appears to be onerous.
Taking into consideration the facts governing,
while  not  interfering  with  that  part  of  the
condition by which the petitioners were directed to
pay  costs  to  the  effect  of  Rs.5  lakhs  to  the
respondent  No.2,  the  remaining  condition  to  pay

--- Page 237 ---
3
costs of Rs.5 lakhs to the Armed Forces Battle
Casualties Welfare Fund, stands modified to a sum of
Rs.1 lakh, which is to be paid within a period of
two weeks from the date of receipt of a copy of this
order.
However, we make it clear that the High Court is
at liberty to proceed with matter expeditiously,
particularly, by taking note of the long pendency of
the matter, preferably within a period of six months
from the date of receipt of a copy of this order.
The Special Leave Petition stands disposed of,
accordingly.
Pending application(s), if any, shall also stand
disposed of.

SPECIAL LEAVE PETITION (CRIMINAL) Diary No. 41723/2026
[Arising  out  of  impugned  final  judgment  and  order  dated
26-08-2025 in CRLAMD No. 182/2023 passed by the High Court of
Judicature at Madras at Madurai]
DINESHKUMAR                                        Petitioner(s)
                                VERSUS
THE STATE OF TAMIL NADU                            Respondent(s)
IA No. 283101/2026 - CONDONATION OF DELAY IN FILING, IA No.
283103/2026 - CONDONATION OF DELAY IN REFILING /  CURING THE
DEFECTS,IA No. 283099/2026 - EXEMPTION FROM FILING C/C OF THE
IMPUGNED JUDGMENT
 
Date : 25-09-2026 This matter was called on for hearing today.
CORAM : 
         HON'BLE MR. JUSTICE J.B. PARDIWALA
         HON'BLE MR. JUSTICE K. VINOD CHANDRAN
For Petitioner(s) :Mr. Sheikh F. Kalia, Adv.
                   Mr. Vignesh A, Adv.
                   Ms. Nithya Sowmya M, Adv.
                   Ms. S. Deepa, Adv.
                   Mr. D.kumanan- 2777, AOR
                   
                   
Diary No. 41723/2026               1

--- Page 272 ---
For Respondent(s) : Mr. S. Gowthaman, AOR
C. Chinnappa, ADv.
Mr. Nilesh Ukey, Adv.
Ms. Shivpa Taneja, Adv.
Mr. Abhisar Thakral, Adv.
Mr. Akash Verma, Adv.
          UPON hearing the counsel the Court made the following
                             O R D E R
1. Delay condoned.
2. Exemption Application is allowed.
3. Leave granted.
4. This appeal has come up today to consider the plea of the
appellant-convict  for  suspension  of  substantive  order  of
sentence of 10 years of rigorous imprisonment and release on
bail, pending the final disposal of his appeal before this
Court.
5. We take notice of the fact that the appellant, accused no. 5
along with co-accused - convicts were put to trial in C.C.No.383
of 2021 for the offence punishable under Sections 8(c) read with
20(b)(ii)(C), 25 and 29(1) of the NDPS Act, 1985, respectively.
6. The contraband involved in the present case is 52 kgs of
Ganja. The appellant and others were held guilty of the alleged
offence by the Trial Court and were sentenced to undergo a fixed
term  of  10  years  of  rigorous  imprisonment  with  fine  of
Rs.1,00,000/-.
7. The appellants went in appeal before the High Court. Their
Diary No. 41723/2026               2

--- Page 273 ---
appeals before the High Court came to be dismissed, thereby
affirming the Judgment and Order of conviction passed by the
Trial Court.
8. We take notice of the fact that the appellant has been
convicted and sentenced for a fixed term of 10 years. He has
already undergone more than 5 years by this time. 
9. Considering the fact that this Court has suspended the
sentence of the similarly placed co-accused, and released them
on bail, we are of the opinion that the sentence imposed on the
appellant can be suspended. 
10. We suspend the substantive order of sentence of 10 years and
order that the appellant be released on bail forthwith, if not
required in any other case, subject to terms and conditions that
the Trial Court may deem fit to impose. 
11. We make it clear that the appellant shall be released after
the amount of fine of Rs.1,00,000/- is deposited by him with the
Trial Court. 
12. Tag with Criminal Appeal No.878/2026.
(CHANDRESH)                                     (POOJA SHARMA)
ASTT. REGISTRAR-cum-PS                        COURT MASTER (NSH)
Diary No. 41723/2026               3

1 of 2

--- Page 310 ---
posted along with Civil Appeal No. 62 of 2018. Apparently, notice
was not issued. Therefore, the Special Leave Petition is listed
today before us along with the Civil Appeal No. 62 of 2018, and
there is no representation of the respondents, naturally as notice
has not been issued. Though, the first respondent in the Civil
Appeal and the Special Leave Petition are one and the same, being
Food  Corporation  of  India  represented  by  Mr.  Abhay  Kumar,
respondent no.2 in the Special Leave Petition, namely Projects and
Equipment Corporation of India Limited remains unrepresented as
notice was not served.

the judgment in the Civil Appeal. Under these circumstances, we
have no option but to direct the petitioner to undertake dasti
notice on respondent no.2. Additionally, a copy of the Special
Leave Petition will also be served on the Central Agency to
represent Projects and Equipment Corporation of India Limited, a
Public Sector Undertaking under the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry.

          UPON hearing the counsel the Court made the following
                             O R D E R
1. The impugned order records that the basis of the Collector’s earlier order
of rejection, impugned in the writ petition, ceases to exist because the appellate
court has set aside the order granting rejection of plaint under Order 7 Rule 11
of the Code of Civil Procedure, 1973 and restored the suit to file. This is followed
by a direction to the Collector to make a reference. This position, on facts, is not
disputed; hence, we see no reason to interfere with the impugned judgment and
order of the High Court. The special leave petition is, accordingly, dismissed.

--- Page 318 ---
2
2. It is, however, clarified that once the reference is made by the Collector,
all contentions / objections may be urged by the parties before the appropriate
court and the same may be decided on its own merits, uninfluenced by any
observation made by the High Court in the impugned order.
3. Pending application(s), if any, shall stand disposed of.

For Respondent(s) :

IA No. 284088/2026 
 
Heard learned counsel for the applicant.
Learned counsel submits that the petitioner is an accused in
Criminal Case No 1345/2024 for committing an offence under Section
138 of the Negotiable Instruments Act was tried by the Vth
Additional Chief Judicial Magistrate, Jam Nagar and vide judgment
and order dated 28.02.2025 the applicant is convicted and sentenced
to undergo simple imprisonment for one year with a direction to pay
1

--- Page 352 ---
compensation of Rs.5,00,000/- to the complainant within stipulated
period of three months commencing from the date of judgment and in
case of default to pay the compensation amount and the accused to
undergo simple imprisonment for six months. The learned counsel
being aggrieved by the judgment and order the petitioner filed an
appeal  and  deposited  20%  of  the  compensation  amount  i.e.
Rs.1,00,000/-. The appeal came to be dismissed and being aggrieved
by the dismissal of the appeal a revision was filed. The learned
counsel  submits  that  a  substantial  ground  is  raised  by  the
petitioner namely the petitioner was deprived of the right of cross
examination of the material witnesses. The learned counsel further
on instructions submits that the petitioner without prejudice to
his rights and contention only to show his bonafide agreed to
deposit further amount as directed by this Court. Considering the
submissions of the learned counsel the petitioner, he is exempted
from surrendering till the first date of listing of the Special
Leave Petition before the Regular Court subject to deposit of Rs.
1,00,000/- within a period of three weeks.
Accordingly, the application for exemption from surrendering
is disposed of.
List the matter before the Hon’ble Court.

ITEM NO.4                  COURT NO.11               SECTION II-A
 
               S U P R E M E  C O U R T  O F  I N D I A
                       RECORD OF PROCEEDINGS
Petition(s) for Special Leave to Appeal (Crl.) No(s).15402/2026
[Arising out of impugned final judgment and order dated 24-06-2026
in CR. MISC. No. 68851/2024 passed by the High Court of Judicature
at Patna]
BIBEKANAND SINHA & ORS.                            Petitioner(s)
                                VERSUS
THE STATE OF BIHAR & ANR.                          Respondent(s)
FOR ADMISSION 
IA No. 247804/2026 - EXEMPTION FROM FILING O.T.
 
Date : 25-09-2026 This matter was called on for hearing today.
CORAM :  HON'BLE MR. JUSTICE PRASHANT KUMAR MISHRA
         HON'BLE MR. JUSTICE SHREE CHANDRASHEKHAR
For Petitioner(s)  Mr. Anurag, AOR
                   Mr. Divyansh Kumar, Adv.
                   Mr. Vivek Ranjan Mohanty, Adv.
                   Ms. Sonal Gupta, Adv.
                   Mr. Hanuman Singh, Adv.                   
                   
For Respondent(s)  Mr. Akshay Amritanshu, AOR
                   Mr. Sarthak Srivastava, Adv. 
          UPON hearing the counsel the Court made the following
                             O R D E R
1. Petitioner(s)’ apprehend arrest in a complaint case
No.165(C) of 2020 pending before the Court of Additional
Chief Judicial Magistrate XI, Patna. The said Court has
issued  summoning  orders  on  03.11.2023  upon  taking
cognizance of the private complaint for offenses under
Section 406, 420, 120B Indian Penal Code, 1860  and under
Section 138 of the Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881.
1

--- Page 448 ---
2. The  petitioner(s)’  anticipatory  bail  petition(s)
have been rejected by the High Court on merits, however,
learned  counsel  for  the  respondent-State  would  place
reliance on ‘Om Prakash Chhawnika v. State of Jharkhand’ 1,
to argue that when only summons have been issued in a
private complaint, there is no apprehension of arrest,
therefore,  application  under  Section  438  of  Code  of
Criminal Procedure, 1973 (Section 482 of Bharatiya Nagarik
Suraksha Sanhita, 2023) is not maintainable.
3. Having  considered  the  rival  submissions  and  on
perusal of the summoning orders passed by the concerned
Magistrate on 03.11.2023, it appears that after taking
cognizance of the complaint, the trial Magistrate has
issued summons to the petitioner(s), and there is no order
for issuance of non-bailable warrant.
4. In view of the above, we dispose of the  Special
Leave  Petition,  directing  the  petitioner(s)  to  appear
before the trial Magistrate, and on such appearance, the
trial Magistrate shall release him on bail on such terms
and conditions as may be imposed by the trial Court.
5. Pending application(s), if any, shall stand disposed
of. 
(MINI)                                      (ANU BHALLA)
COURT MASTER (SH)                             COURT MASTER (NSH)
1 .  2026 SCC OnLine SC 676
2

--- Page 449 ---

parties. It is stated that the parties are interested in having
a mediated settlement to the disputes between them.

matter to the Mediation Centre attached to the High Court of
Madras. The parties shall appear before the Coordinator of the
concerned Mediation Centre on 13 th October, 2026 at 11:00 a.m.

nominated by the Coordinator to allow virtual appearance of the
parties or to insist on physical presence as may be necessary.

5. List the matter after the mediation report is received.

For Respondent(s)  
         UPON hearing the counsel the Court made the following
                             O R D E R
1. Learned counsel for the petitioners filed an application
bearing I.A. No.253965 of 2026 seeking permission for substituted
service upon the unserved respondent Nos. 1 to 4, by way of paper
publication in a National English Newspaper i.e., ‘The Indian
Express’ and in a vernacular local daily i.e., ‘Kannada Prabha’.
2. The said application is allowed.
3. List on 04.11.2026.
4. In the meantime, learned counsel for the petitioners may file
proof of service.
